# 수문 및 기상 관측용 강수량계 규격 국제 표준화

### International standardization for specifications of hydrological and meteorological rainfall gauge

2020. 11

# 제	출	문

사 장 귀하

이 보고서를 2020년도 K-water연구원 물에너지연구소에서 수행한 “수문 및 기상 관측용 강수량계 규격 국제 표준화”의 최종 연구보고서로 제출합니다.

2020. 11\. 30

연구책임자 : K-water연구원 책임연구원 홍성택

연구수행자 : K-water연구원 수석연구원 신강욱

K-water연구원 수석연구원 황만하

K-water연구원 전자통신3급 김학성

K-water연구원 책임위원 이호현

K-water연구원 책임연구원 이상진

K-water연구원 전자통신4급 김국일

K-water연구원 연구원 김일한

인재개발원 주 임 앤드류

<tr><td colspan="12">◦ 기술적 파급효과<br>- 창조경제를 육성하고, 경제를 혁신하기 위해서는 과학기술에 기반한 융합 신산업이 요구되는 바, 강수량계의 국제 표준화는 ICT 기술과 기상기술이 융합된<br>기술 분야로서 재해 예방, 대응에 필수적인 탐지 기술을 개발하는 것으로 미래에 중추적인 신산업으로 발전할 가능성이 높음<br>◦ 경제․산업적 측면<br>- 기상이변과 기후변화에 대응하는 일은 국정과제에 포함되는 국가 전략적인 목표에 해당하며, 제3차 과학기술기본계획(’13∼’17)에서 나타난 것처럼 기후변화 등으로 인한 자연재해에 선제적으로 대응함으로써 피해를 최소화하기 위해서는 자연재해 모니<br>터링/예측/대응기술이 가장 시급하게 필요함<br>- 미국, 일본, 유럽 등 선진 각국에서도 자연재해에 대한 정책적 대응을 지속적으로 추진하고<br>있음<br>- 악천후 관측, 재난재해 예방 기술은 국민의 안전을 위해 반드시 필요한 분야이나 현재는 민간 시장이 발달하지 않은 분야이므로 정부 주도로 개발하는 것이 타당함<br>- 미국 등 선진국의 경우 국가기술로서 중요성이 높은 경우 경제성이 충분하지 않더라도<br>지속적으로 투자함으로써 원천 기술을 확보하는데 주력하고 있음<br>◦ 사회․문화적 측면<br>- 집중호우, 폭설, 태풍 등은 매년 반복되고 있으며 이로 인해 도심침수, 범람, 산사태, 산간<br>마을 고립, 도시/교통로 마비 등이 발생하는 바, 본 기술은 악천후 감시를 통해 국민 체감도가 높은 이러한 각종 자연재해의 피해 발생을 억제할 수 있는 기술로서 기술 개발이<br>시급함</td></tr>
</table>

- vi -

# 요	약	문

1. 연구제목

수문 및 기상 관측용 강수량계 규격 국제 표준화

2. 연구기간
2020. 1\. 1 \~ 2020. 11. 30
3. 연구목적

최근 전 세계적으로 지구온난화에 따른 기후변화로 홍수 및 가뭄 등 수재해 발생 빈도가 높아짐에 따라 국내 지자체 및 동남아(태국, 필리핀), 아프리카(알제리) 등 해외에서 홍수재해 통합관리 등 수재해 관리에 대한 관심이 증가되고 있다.

강수량계는 기상청, 농진청, 산림청, 국토부 및 각 지자체 등에서 강우량을 측정하여 농어촌 및 산림녹화 안전관리 등에 활용하고 있으며, 비의 량을 기록 관리하여 차년도 등에 예측 활용할 수 있는 등 사용분야가 광범위하지만 국가표준(KS) 및 국제표준(ISO)이 없어 표준화가 필요한 실정이다.

4. 연구내용

| 연구 내용 및 범위 | 연구 수행 성과 |
| --- | --- |
| ㅇ수문 및 기상 관측용 강수량계 규격 ISO DIS 승인<br>ㅇ수문 및 기상 관측용 강수량계 규격 ISO CD 승인<br>ㅇISO TC 146 SC5 정기총회 참석(온라인 회의)<br>ㅇISO DIS 제정을 위한 기술워크숍 개최<br>ㅇ강수량계 CMC 산출을<br>위한 전문가 세미나 개최<br>ㅇISO/DIS-23350 기술전문가 세미나 개최<br>ㅇISO/CD-23435 적설계<br>전문가 자문회의 개최 | ㅇISO/DIS-23350(집수형 액체 강수량 측정장치)<br>DIS 제출 및 승인<br>ㅇISO/CD-23435(적설계 시험방법) CD 제출 및 승인<br>ㅇISO/AWI-23334(강우관측소 밀도기준) PIW<br>Project Leader 교체<br>ㅇISO TC 146 SC5 정기총회 참석(온라인 회의)<br>ㅇISO 국제표준 제정을 위한 TC113/SC5,<br>TC146/SC5 국제전문가 협업<br>ㅇISO DIS 및 CD 승인을 위한 국제전문가 협업(이<br>메일 송수신)<br>ㅇISO/DIS-23350(집수형 액체 강수량 측정장치)<br>DIS 코멘트 보완 회의 개최<br>ㅇ기술워크숍: 강수량계 기술 ISO(국제표준) 제정<br>ㅇ자문회의: 강수량계 CMC 산출 및 교정절차 검토 외 2건<br>ㅇ논문게재 및 발표: 각 1건 |

- vii -
5. 연구결과

수문 및 기상 관측용 강수량계 국제 표준화를 위하여 ISO 국제표준 채택을 위하여 TC 113/SC 5 분야에 DIS_23350)집수형 액체 강수량 측정장치)에 대한 DIS 승인, TC 146/SC 5 분야에 CD_23435(적설계 시험방법)에 대한 CD 승인, TC 113 분야에 AWI_23334(강우관측소 밀도기준)에 대한 프로젝트 리더 변경, ISO 국제 영상 회의 참석, ISO 제정을 위한 기술 워크샵 개최, 수문 및 기상분야 전문가 회의 등 다음과 같은 내용을 수행하였다.

□ ISO/DIS-23350(집수형 액체 강수량 측정장치) DIS 투표회람 및 승인

◦ DIS-23350(집수형 액체 강수량 측정장치)

- 표준위원회 : ISO/TC 113/SC 5(Instruments, equipment and data management)
- 명	칭 : Catching-type Liquid Precipitation Measuring Gauges

(집수형 액체 강수량 측정장치)

- 표준화내용 : 1) 강수량계 분류, 설치조건 WMO 기준 준용, 2) 계측기 규격(일반 사항·규격, 동작범위), 3) 기록(형식·간격), 4) 케이스 및 설치방법, 5) 불확도(A형, B형, 합성불확도) - 추진 현황 및 향후 계획
- · ‘16. 06. \~ ‘17. 12. : NP 작성 및 국제제안(ISO/TC 113)
- · ‘18. 01. \~ ‘18. 04. : NP 회람 및 투표, 승인(ISO/TC 113/SC 5)
- · ‘18. 04. \~ ‘18. 12. : WD 작성 및 제출(K-water, 국제전문가)
- · ‘19. 01. \~ ‘19. 04. : CD 회람 및 투표, 승인
- · ‘19. 04. \~ ’20. 04. : CD 코멘트 보완( 및 DIS(안) 제출
- · ‘20. 05. \~ ’20. 09. : DIS 회람 및 투표, 승인
- · ‘20. 09 \~ ‘20. 12. : DIS 코멘트 보완, FDIS 회람 및 승인
- · ‘21. 01 \~ ‘21. 04. : 국제표준(IS) 최종 발간
- 세부 내용 :

· (기술조건) 강수량계 분류, 측정능력(분해능, 강우강도에 따른 측정범위, 정확도), 수수구(형태, 직경, 재질) 제시

· (온도조건) 작동온도 : -40℃ \~ +60 ℃, 히팅(無) : 0℃ \~ +60 ℃

저장온도 : -20℃ \~ +40 ℃

· (데이터형식) 연월일 : 8자리, 시분초 : 8자리, 강우강도 : 5자리 이상

· (기록간격) 기록식 : 계측 즉시 전송, 비기록식 : 지정된 간격마다 수동 기록

· (외형,설치) 외함 재질, IP67 방진/방수 보호 규격, 설치방법

· (측정불확도) 측정불확도 요소, A형 및 B형 불확도 평가,

데이터 불확도에 의한 합성불확도 산출 방안 제시

- 투표 대상 : ISO/TC 113/SC 5 P 멤버국(17개국 : Australia, Austria, China, Czech Republic, Egypt, France, Germany, India, Japan, Korea, Republic of, Netherlands, Norway, Romania, Sweden, Switzerland, United Kingdom, United States)
- 승인조건 : 투표한 P멤버 2/3 찬성, 투표한 모든 회원국 1/4 반대
- DIS 투표 회람(문서번호 : N 609)

□ ISO/CD-23435(적설계 시험방법) CD 투표 회람 및 승인

◦ CD-23435(적설계 시험방법)

- 표준화 내용
- 표준위원회 : ISO/TC 146/SC 5(Meteorology)
- 명	칭 : Test methods for snow depth sensors(적설계 시험방법)
- 표준화내용 : 적설 표면에서부터 센서까지의 거리를 측정하는 적설계에 대한 요구사항 및 시험방법에 관한 표준안 제시
- 추진 현황 및 향후 계획
- · ‘16. 06. \~ ‘18. 03. : NP 작성 및 국제제안(ISO/TC 146)
- · ‘18. 03. \~ ’18. 09. : NP 회람 및 투표, 채택(ISO/TC 146/SC 5)
- · ‘18. 10.	: ISO/TC 146/SC 5 WG 10 설립 및 Convenor, Project Leader 수임
- · ‘18. 10. \~ ’20. 05. : WD 작성 및 제출
- · ‘20. 05. \~ ’20. 07. : CD 회람 및 투표, 승인
- · ‘20. 08. \~ ’21. 06. : CD 코멘트 보완 및 DIS(안) 제출
- · ‘21. 07. \~ ’21. 09. : DIS 국제표준(안) 회람 및 투표
- · ‘21. 10. \~ ’22. 06. : DIS 코멘트 보완, FDIS 회람 및 승인
- · ‘22. 07. \~ ’22. 09. : 국제표준(IS) 최종 발간
- 세부 내용 :

· (기능시험) : 분해능, 정확도 및 측정 범위 등 성능 테스트

· (온도시험) : 챔버의 온도 변화를 통한 적설계 동작 범위 시험

· (풍동시험) : 실제 속도(벡터)와 측정된 풍속의 편차의 동작 범위 결정

· (설정시험) : 자동/수동 설정을 통한 풍속 및 동작 온도 범위 결정

· (현장시험) : 실험실에서 시뮬레이션하기 어려운 환경 조건에 대한 시험

· (기 준 면) : 적설판 및 자연지면

◦ 일본 및 중국 국제전문가 협업을 통한 CD(안) 작성 및 투표 회람

3. 수행 내용 및 범위

- 수문 및 기상 관측용 강수량계 규격 ISO DIS 승인

- 수문 및 기상 관측용 강수량계 규격 ISO CD 승인

- ISO TC 146 SC5 정기총회 참석(온라인 회의)

- ISO DIS 제정을 위한 기술워크숍 개최

- 강수량계 CMC 산출을 위한 전문가 세미나 개최

- ISO/DIS-23350 기술전문가 세미나 개최

- ISO/CD-23435 적설계 전문가 자문회의 개최

4. 과제 수행 성과

- ISO/DIS-23350(집수형 액체 강수량 측정장치) DIS 제출 및 승인

- ISO/CD-23435(적설계 시험방법) CD 제출 및 승인

- ISO/AWI-23334(강우관측소 밀도기준) PIW Project Leader 교체

- ISO TC 146 SC5 정기총회 참석(온라인 회의)

- ISO 국제표준 제정을 위한 TC113/SC5, TC146/SC5 국제전문가 협업

- ISO DIS 및 CD 승인을 위한 국제전문가 협업(이메일 송수신)

- ISO/DIS-23350(집수형 액체 강수량 측정장치) DIS 코멘트 보완 회의 개최

- 기술워크숍: 강수량계 기술 ISO(국제표준) 제정

- 자문회의: 강수량계 CMC 산출 및 교정절차 검토

ISO/DIS-23350 기술전문가 자문회의

ISO/CD-23435 적설계 전문가 자문회의

- 논문발표: 측정환경에 따른 강수량계 측정불확도 분석(한국통신학회)

- 논문게재: 가속도계를 이용한 탄성파 기반 누수탐지 기술 분석(한국정보통신학회)

5. 활용 방안 및 기대 효과

- 국제표준 추진으로 기상 측정기술 위상 제고 및 기상산업 시장 활성화

- 표준화된 정확한 강수량 측정으로 재산 및 인명피해 최소화

- 홍수예보 정확도 향상시 침수로 인한 피해 저감액을 약 20%로 추정할 경우 연평균 1.6조원의 피해액을 기준으로 3,200억원 \~ 4,000억원의 경제적 효과발생(최우정, 2011: 미래부, 2013)

## 제1장 사업개요

### 제1절 목표 및 내용

1. 협약 목표
- 수문 및 기상 관측용 강수량계 규격 국제표준 DIS 채택 1건 이상
- 수문 및 기상 관측용 강수량계 규격 KS 제정 1건 이상
- 국제 표준 개발을 위한 국내외 기술표준 워크샵 및 세미나 개최 (연간 각 1회 이상)

### File: ISO_DIS 23350

ISO/DIS 23350

ISO TC 113/SC 05/WG 7

Secretariat: ANSI

## Hydrometry—catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauges

# DIS stage

Warning for WDs and CDs

This document is not an ISO International Standard. It is distributed for review and comment. It is subject to change without notice and may not be referred to as an International Standard.

Recipients of this draft are invited to submit, with their comments, notification of any relevant patent rights of which they are aware and to provide supporting documentation.

### Contents

Introduction 	 3

1 Scope 	 4

2 Normative references 	 4

3 Terms and definitions 	 4

4 Instrument specifications 	 7

5 Recording 	 9

6 Environmental protection and housing 	 10

7 Installation 	 10

8 Estimation of measurement uncertainties 	 11

Annex A (Types of precipitation gauges) 	 13

Bibliography 	 21

### Introduction

This Standard covers instrumentation that measures rainfall intensity (RI) and liquid precipitation by collecting the volume of water that falls on a fixed area. The collected volume of water is divided by the fixed area of the collecting device to give a value for volume per unit area, termed rainfall depth, and measured in mm×h-1 or kg×m-2. Unlike many other meteorological instruments, there is no absolute physical standard against which a raingauge (hereafter called catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauges) can be compared. Many different types, shapes, and sizes of scatching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauges are acceptable for the measurement of rainfall and rainfall depth, each reflecting a specific requirement. Most consist of a circular collecting device, delineating the fixed area of the sample, and a funnel leading into a storage reservoir and/or measuring system. Some types of automatic gauges do not require a funnel. Since various sizes and shapes of orifice and gauge heights are used in different countries, the measurements are not strictly comparable.

NOTE 1 This standard specifies only the functional requirements of catching-type liquid precipitation

measuring gauges, and does not include specification, design and installation conditions.

(REFERENCE: BS 7843-3:2012, Introduction)

(REFERENCE: WMO-No.:2012, CH 6.1.2 & CH 6.1.4.1)

(REFERENCE: BS EN 17277:2019)

## Hydrometry—catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauges

### 1 Scope

This International Standard specifies the typical requirements of instrumentation for measuring liquid precipitation, primarily for the purpose of hydrological and meteorological observation. Its scope is limited to both non-recording and recording catching-type precipitation gauges for the measurement of liquid precipitation. The standard covers design criteria for the gauges and elements of uncertainty that need to be considered in their construction.

This International Standard is supplemented by an annex providing guidance on the types of catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauges currently available and the measurement uncertainty associated with them (see Annex A).

### 2 Normative references

The following referenced documents are indispensable for the application of this document. For dated references, only the edition cited applies. For undated references, the latest edition of the referenced document (including any amendments) applies.

ISO 772:2011, Hydrometry — Vocabulary and symbols

ISO/IEC Guide 98-3:2008, Guide to the expression of uncertainty in measurement (GUM:1995)

JCGM 200:2012 (E/F) International guide of metrology – Basic and general concepts and associated terms (VIM) 3rd Edition: 2008

### 3 Terms and definitions

For the purpose of this document, the terms and definitions given in ISO 772, BS 7843-3 and ISO/IEC Guide 98-3 apply.

## 3.1 accuracy( of measurement)

qualitative expression for the closeness of a measured value to the true value

NOTE 1 The quantitative expression of accuracy should be in terms of uncertainty. Good accuracy implies small random and systematic errors.

(REFERENCE: ISO 772:2011, CH 7.38)

## 3.2 calibration

process which establishes, under specified conditions, the relationship between the values indicated by a catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauge and the corresponding known values indicated by a measurement standard with associated measurement uncertainties.

(REFERENCE: BS 7843-3:2012, CH 3.1)

3.3 error of measurement

result of a measurement minus a true value of the measurand

NOTE 1 The term relates equally to

— the uncorrected result, and

## — the corrected result.

NOTE 2 The known parts of the error of measurement may be compensated by applying the appropriate correction. The error of the corrected result

(REFERENCE: ISO 772:2011, CH 7.24)

## 3.4 precipitation (liquid and solid)

precipitation is defined as the liquid or solid products of the condensation of water vapour falling from clouds or deposited from the air onto the ground. It includes rain, hail, snow, dew, rime, hoar frost, and fog precipitation

## ( REFERENCE: WMO No.8 :2012, CH 6.1.1)

## 3.5 rain measure

graduated measuring cylinder made of clear glass or plastic, used by the observer for measuring the volume of collected liquid and melted solid precipitation

## ( REFERENCE: BS 7843-3:2012, CH 3.10)

## 3.6 rainfall

total liquid component of precipitation, including condensation from the atmosphere, collected and measured by a catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauge

( REFERENCE: BS 7843-3:2012, CH 3.7)

3.7 rainfall intensity

accumulated precipitation in unit time

## ( REFERENCE: BS 7843-3:2012, CH 3.9)

3.8 raingauge

an instrument that measures rainfall

## 3.8.1 automatic raingauge

collecting raingauge that measures rainfall by automatic means

( REFERENCE: BS 7843-3:2012, CH 3.11.1)

## 3.8.2 collecting raingauge

instrument that collects precipitation falling through an orifice of the known cross-sectional area for the measurement of its water equivalent volume, mass or weight accumulated over a measured period

( REFERENCE: BS 7843-3:2012, CH 3.11.2)

## 3.8.3 storage raingauge

collecting raingauge that accumulates rainfall and melted solid precipitation in a collecting vessel for manual measurement of its volume

## ( REFERENCE: BS 7843-3:2012, CH 3.11.3)

## 3.9 resolution

quantitative expression of the ability of an indication device to distinguish meaningfully between closely adjacent values of the quantity indicated

## (REFERENCE: ISO 772:2011 CH 7.1)

## 3.10 uncertainty

estimate characterizing the range of values within which the true value of a measurand lies

(REFERENCE: ISO 772:2011 CH 7.35)

## 3.10.1 combined uncertainty

standard uncertainty of the result of a measurement when that result is obtained from the values of a number of other quantities, equal to the positive square root of a sum of terms, the terms being the variances or covariances of these other quantities weighted according to how the measurement result varies with changes in these quantities

(REFERENCE: ISO 772:2011 CH 7.43)

## 3.10.2 expanded uncertainty

expanded uncertainty quantity defining an interval about the result of a measurement that may be expected to encompass a large fraction of the values that could be attributed to the measurand

(REFERENCE: ISO 772:2011 CH 7.42)

## 3.10.3 type A evaluation of uncertainty

evaluation of a component of measurement uncertainty by a statistical analysis of measured quantity values obtained under defined measurement conditions

(REFERENCE: ISO 772:2011 CH 7.40)

## 3.10.4 type B evaluation of uncertainty

evaluation of a component of measurement uncertainty determined by means other than a type A evaluation of measurement uncertainty

(REFERENCE: ISO 772:2011 CH 7.41)

## 3.10.5 standard uncertainty

uncertainty of the result of a measurement expressed as a standard deviation

## (REFERENCE: ISO 772:2011 CH 7.39)

### 4 Instrument specifications

### 4.1 General

Catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauges are classified as non-recording and recording types. The non-recording type has ordinary and storage gauges. The recording type has weighting, tipping-bucket, and floating types. It usually has a resolution of 0.1 mm to 1 mm, and the measuring rainfall intensities in the range 0.1 to 1,000 mm×h-1 or higher. Measurement errors may occur according to installation conditions, measurement environment, and solid precipitation.

The catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauge shall be made of circular orifice and shall be formed perpendicular to the outer slope and the inner surface with sharp edges. Edge diameters shall be less than 0.2 percent of diameter in any direction. The surface of the funnel shall consist of a stable, durable material such that the water drop is not maintained by surface tension, is freely moved towards the orifice and is passed to the measuring mechanism.

NOTE 1 Although this standard specifies the functional requirements of catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauges, consideration of the siting and exposure of gauges is important. More specifically, the installation conditions, measurement environment, and solid precipitation are well documented in the references below.

(REFERENCE: BS 7843-3:2012, CH 7.2.3)

(REFERENCE: BS 7843-3:2012, CH 6.1.5)

(REFERENCE: WMO CIMO_Guide-7th_Edition-2008-WMO No 8, CH 6)

### 4.2 General gauge specifications

The gauge manufacturer should provide general specifications that may impact the measurement of precipitation, these include but are not limited to:

a) Measurement range for precipitation intensity or total rainfall volume

b) Resolution

c) Accuracy

d) Size of orifice area

e) Standardized communication interfaces and protocols for data transmission

f) Power supply and consumption

g) Height of gauge (including rim height placement to avoid interference from deposited snow or other materials near gauge)

### 4.3 Environment

### 4.3.1 General

Liquid precipitation measuring gauges shall operate within the ranges of temperature in 4.3.2 and the ranges of relative humidity in 4.3.3.

NOTE 1 Wind should be taken into consideration in areas that are prone to inclement weather conditions

(REFERENCE: WMO Guide to Meteorological Instruments and Methods of Observation, PART 1 )

### 4.3.2 Temperature

Cathing-type liquid precipitation measuring gauges shall operate within the following temperature range:

a) Operating temperature

- Maximum temperature: +60 °C

- Minimum temperature (catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauges, excluding heating or weighing gauge without antifreeze): 0 °C

- Minimum temperature (catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauge, with heating or weighing gauge with antifreeze): –40 °C

b) Storage temperature

- Maximum temperature: +40 °C

- Minimum temperature: –20 °C

( REFERENCE: BS 7843-3:2012, CH 11.1\~11.2)

### 4.3.3 Relative humidity

Catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauges shall operate within the following relative humidity range:

a) Operating relative humidity

- Maximum relative humidity: 100 % RH

- Minimum relative humidity: 0 % RH

b) Storage relative humidity

- Maximum relative humidity: 80% RH

- Minimum relative humidity: 0% RH

( REFERENCE: BS 7843-3:2012, CH 11.1\~ 11.2)

### 5 Recording

### 5.1 Recording format

For recording and non-recording type liquid precipitation gauges, the length of each record to be stored in a data storage gauge or manual recording shall be at least eight digits for the year, month and day, eight digits for the hour, minute, second and millisecond (e.g. 2019/12/31 09:00:00:00). For rainfall intensity (RI), there shall be at least five digits (e.g.

00.000 mm×h-1), not including the decimal place, for the liquid precipitation amount which is measured in mm×h-1 or kg×m-2.

### 5.2 Recording interval

The recording interval must be specified by the network operator to ensure regulatory compliance and fit with regional and climatological constraints.

For recording type gauges, the measurement record of liquid precipitation should be transmitted to the recording device instantaneously or at a specified time according to the gauge user.

Non-recording type liquid precipitation gauges should be read manually at a specified interval to acquire the data.

Ordinary and Storage gauges should be emptied in line with network operating principles, commonly daily or monthly depending on gauge design and network purpose. Liquid precipitation is reported per unit of time, the manufacturer or network operator should specify the volume of liquid precipitation and time interval recorded in its most granular form.

### 6 Environmental protection and housing

The catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauge should be constructed in a material that is durable and can endure natural weathering without changes to its surface characteristics. Sound watertight seams should be used throughout. The collecting funnel should fit firmly over the top of the measurement mechanism but should be removable without undue force. The gauge should be designed to minimize ingress by small animals and insects. The outer housing of the gauge should also prevent water from entering the measuring component of the gauge, apart from through the inlet funnel. Electrical connections for power supplies and outgoing signals should be provided using water-resistant plugs or terminals, suitable for the environmental conditions in which the gauge is deployed. Ingress Protection Code 67 (IP67) is suitable for this. Any marking identifying connectors should be weather-resistant and remain legible for the expected lifetime of the catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauge.

(REFERENCE: IEC 60529:Edition 2.2 2013-08, CH 13\~14)

### 7 Installation

The manufacturer shall provide clear instructions for the installation of catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauges.

NOTE 1 Although outside the scope of this standard, it should be noted that measurements will be influenced by rim height either through individual gauge design or local deployment to allow for factors such as snow depth where close to ground level deployment is not practical. All gauge types should be sited to ensure operation within the manufacturer’s design.

The rim of the gauge should be installed parallel to the ground surface. The design should maintain plane of rim level to within ±2° of the base plane. Failure to ensure that the rim is level will introduce a systematic error into measurement. The measurement mechanism of the gauge, e.g. a tipping bucket mechanism, also needs to be level to ensure there are no systematic errors.

The gauge should be installed securely to ensure that it remains stable. This must take into account impacts such as frost heave and changes in ground conditions.

The gauge should be sited to ensure that the surrounding surface minimises splashback that can result in an over-catch. A surface of gravel or short grass is typical and surfaces such as concrete should be avoided.

(REFERENCE: BS 7843-3:2012, CH 6.1.4)

### 8 Estimation of measurement uncertainty

### 8.1 General

The uncertainty of a value derived from primary measurements may be due to

a) Signal Noise which affects the value being measured (variation by the precipitation intensity), or

b) Resolution of the measurement process.

Two methods of estimation, Type A and Type B, are described in the Guide to the Expression of Uncertainty in Measurement (GUM 1995) for relating the dispersion of values to the probability of “closeness” to the mean value.

NOTE 1 Although installation and environmental factors are major sources of uncertainties, they are outside the scope

### 8.2 Factors of measurement uncertainty

In practice, there are many possible sources of uncertainty in a measurement, including a) personal bias in reading

b) finite instrument resolution or discrimination threshold

c) variations in repeated observations of the measurand

(REFERENCE: GUM ISO/IEC Guide 98-3:2008, CH 3.3)

### 8.3 Type-A estimation

It is the method of evaluation of uncertainty by the statistical analysis of series of observations. It stems from manual reading and evaporation and wetting of the instrument.

(REFERENCE: GUM ISO/IEC Guide 98-3:2008, CH 2.3)

### 8.4 Type-B estimation

It is the method of evaluation of uncertainty by means other than the statistical analysis of series of observations. It stems from resolution, aging, and random error of the instrument.

(REFERENCE: GUM ISO/IEC Guide 98-3:2008, CH 2.3)

### 8.5 Data uncertainty

A precipitation measurement is not an absolute measurement. It is always relative to data, for example, atmospheric pressure, water temperature, and other factors. The uncertainty associated with the data should be combined with the uncertainty of the derived value.

(REFERENCE: ISO 4373:2008(E), CH 8.4)

### 8.6 Combined uncertainty

To determine the uncertainty of the derived value, U, it is necessary to combine the uncertainties of all primary measurements, u, thus,

$_{U(≺ipitation)=}\sqrt{u(≺ipitationdata)^{2}+u(≺ipitationmeasurement)^{2}}$

This illustrates the method when combining the uncertainty of a precipitation data value. Other components of measurement uncertainty are added by inclusion of their squared value within the brackets.

(REFERENCE: GUM ISO/IEC Guide 98-3:2008, CH 5)

### Annex A

liquid Catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauges

### A.1 Non-recording liquid gauges

### A.1.1 Ordinary gauge

### A.1.1.1 Explanation

Normally, an ordinary gauge is composed of the collector orifice with an area with 200 cm2 to 500 cm2, a storage with a funnel shape, and a bucket. Storing rain or solid precipitation during the observation hours, the graduated cylinder shall be used to measure the accumulated precipitation.

Key

1 Funnel

2 Measuring gauge

Figure A.1 — Ordinary gauge

### A.1.1.2 Materials

An ordinary gauge is made of various types of metallic or plastic materials with proper thermal expansion coefficient. They must be manufactured to minimize errors due to wetting loss and evaporation.

(REFERENCE: WMO CIMO_Guide-7th_Edition-2008-WMO No 8, CH 6.3.1.1)

![image](image_032.png)

### A.1.1.3 Advantages

It is easy to install and measure and is available to measure precipitation accurately at a low cost.

### A.1.1.4 Disadvantages

The non-recording gauge must be read manually and cannot determine the exact start and end of the precipitation occurrence. The timing cannot be determined between individual events within the recording period. Therefore, unmanned observation or remote recording is not possible. Uncertainty factors exist due to measurement time and scale indication. Evaporation may result in under reading, particularly if measurements are made less frequently than daily. If a non-recording gauge is used for solid precipitation measurements, the funnel shall be removed in winter and the readings may be inaccurate.

NOTE 1 Although the measurement of solid type precipitation is outside the scope, it should be noted that it is a disadvantage that applies to all gauges mentioned in the annex.

### A.1.1.5 Uncertainties

A triangular distribution applies to the uncertainty, u, associated with reading an ordinary gauge, x, so that

Ordinary gauges have a triangular uncertainty distribution, u, so that Equation (A.1) applies.

$\frac{1}{\sqrt{6}}_{\times}\frac{(xmax-xmin)}{2}$

$u_{x}=$	(A.1)

where

$x_{max}$ is the discernible upper limit;

$xmin$ is the discernible lower limit.

EXAMPLE If, from inspection, the discernible upper limit is 100 mm and the discernible lower limit is 99.9 mm, then the best estimate is 99.95 mm with an uncertainty of 0.0204 mm.

### A.1.2 Storage gauge

### A.1.2.1 Explanation

A storage gauge either measures the weight or reads the graduated cylinder of a bucket after collecting rainwater in the bucket for a certain period such as measuring the total precipitation in a remote area.

The gauge is composed of a collecting part with a funnel and a storage part that can hold enough precipitation between scheduled readings. Proper oil of around 5 mm shall be typically put in a measuring bottle and a removable inner can for over spill to prevent the evaporation of water. This oil shall have a property to pass through collected water freely to prevent evaporation. In the winter season, antifreeze shall be mixed to measure the solid precipitation in the liquid state.

If a storage gauge is used for solid precipitation measurements, the funnel shall be removed in winter and the readings may be inaccurate.

When the location of a storage gauge is located far from the observer, the gauge requires special care in terms of operation and management. For example, the installation of a gauge may be needed to monitor abnormal conditions like clogging of the gauge entrance by impurities.

Key

1 Funnel

2 Bucket

Figure A.2 — Storage gauge

### A.1.2.2 Materials

Storage gauges are made of various types of metallic or plastic materials with proper thermal expansion coefficient. They must be manufactured to minimize the loss of water by the wet error and evaporation.

### A.1.2.3 Advantages

This type of gauge is easy to install and measure, and is available to measure accurate precipitation at a low cost.

### A.1.2.4 Disadvantages

As it is not possible to measure in real-time and there exist many cases of poor accessibility, the gauge requires monitoring to check if the the funnel is plugged In addition, this type of gauge requires oil or antifreeze to prevent evaporation, but such substances may cause environmental pollution.

### A.1.2.5 Uncertainties

A triangular distribution applies to the uncertainty associated with reading a storage gauge, so that Equation (A.1) applies.

![image](image_033.png)

EXAMPLE If, from the measurement, the discernable upper limit is 100 mm and the lower limit is 99.9 mm, then the best estimate is 99.95 mm and its uncertainty is 0.0204 mm.

### A.2 Recording gauges

As the catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauge with automatic recording has a higher time resolution than manual measurements, it is possible to reduce evaporation and wetting loses.

Recording gauges generally used are the weighing type, tipping-bucket type, and floating type. Some weighing gauges can measure all types of precipitation without the need for additional heaters. Other recording-type liquid precipitation measuring gauges will require heaters fitted to the rim and/or funnel if they are required to measure frozen precipitation.

Newer recording gauges measure precipitation without any moving parts by fixed type. This type of gauge also consists of a condenser gauge, atmospheric pressure converter, and optic or small radar gauge; and provides electronic signals that are generated in proportion to the precipitation intensity. In addition, an important component of the recording gauge is the clock gauge which is essential for providing information on the time intervals, recording day and time.

### A.2.1 Weighing gauge

### A.2.1.1 Explanation

A weighing gauge measures the weight continuously by a weight sensor or a load cell when rain or solid precipitation enters through the upper part of the receiver and fills the collection bucket that is placed inside the casing. The gauge consists of a collection bucket to receive water and a gauge to measure weight. The weighing gauge can measure precipitation in the form of solid precipitation. Its operation has an advantage in that unnecessary special preparation processes besides emptying the water in the collection bucket are not required. Yet, a method is required to minimize evaporation such as mixing oil or other suppressants inside the collection bucket to form a layer of film. Evaporation doesn’t necessarily need to be prevented if the gauge software takes into account the weight of the gauge when making calculations.

![image](image_034.png)

Key 1 Receiving funnel

2 Collection bucket

3 Balance

4 Casing

Figure A.3 — Weighing gauge

### A.2.1.2 Materials

The exterior of the gauge should be manufactured with a durable and corrosion-free material like stainless steel which is commonly used. It is important to review the reliability of power supply since the gauge records measurements automatically.

### A.2.1.3 Advantages

It is useful to measure both liquid and solid type precipitation such as snow, sleet, and hail. This gauge provides precise measurements with fine resolution.

### A.2.1.4 Disadvantages

If the precipitation accumulated in the bucket exceeds the capacity of bucket, it shall be discharged automatically or manually. The automatic discharge type does not use antifreeze for its operation, but the manual discharge type needs antifreeze to minimize water inside the bucket from freezing. In addition, in case of manual discharge type, the capacity of the bucket must be large and it needs an evaporation suppressor or the gauge software.

The weighing gauge is not suitable to discern the types of precipitation. Errors may occur as the load is affected by the strong wind pressure.

NOTE 1 Although solid type precipitation is outside the scope, it should be noted that the uncertainty of tthe measurement of solid type precipitation will increasebe less acccurate.

### A.2.1.5 Uncertainties

If a weighing gauge is used, then the rectangular uncertainty distribution is likely so that …

(A.2)

$u_{x}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\times \frac{x}{2}$

where

$x$ is the resolution

EXAMPLE If, from measurement, the discernible upper limit is 100 mm and the discernible lower limit is 99.9 mm, then the best estimate is 99.95 mm with an uncertainty of 0.0289 mm.

(REFERENCE: WMO Instruments and Observing Methods Report No. 99, CH 2.1)

### A.2.2 Tipping bucket gauge

### A.2.2.1 Explanation

Tipping bucket gauges measure the rate and accumulated total of precipitation. Water collected in the receiver fills one of two small buckets with a certain amount of water. Once a sufficient amount of water is collected in the bucket, it tips over on one side due to the weight of the water and the rainwater is drained. At this time, an electric circuit which is connected to a remote terminal unit generates an electric signal that produces a record.

Key

1 Funnel

2 Tipping bucket

3 Drain cylinder

4 Reed-relay switch

5 Calibration adjuster

Figure A.4 — Tipping bucket gauge

### A.2.2.2 Materials

The gauge should be manufactured with a durable and corrosion-free material like stainless steel.

### A.2.2.3 Advantages

The gauge is relatively simple. As it is available for remote measurements and easy to produce digitalized observation signals, the gauge has been widely used for automatic weather stations. It is suitable to measure the accumulated sum and rate of precipitation.

![image](image_035.png)

### A.2.2.4 Disadvantages

The gauge has some limitations in catching high-intensity precipitation and if the precipitation intensity increases, the accuracy of observed values declines. Although negligible, there is a possibility of evaporation loss in hot regions. It is impossible for the gauge to measure if counting elements fail such as a reed switch, etc. If it is a heated type gauge errors are possible due to the evaporation of water collected.

Although the time is short when the water is poured out from the bucket and returns to its original position, a small amount of precipitation could be missed during that time. Therefore, if a severe rainstorm occurs, a nonlinear error occurs so the precision of the gauge cannot be guaranteed.

(REFERENCE: WMO CIMO_Guide-7th_Edition-2008-WMO No 8, CH 6.5.2)

### A.2.2.5 Uncertainties

Tipping bucket gauges have a rectangular uncertainty distribution so that Equation (A.2) applies.

EXAMPLE If from the measurement of a precipitation gauge with 0.1 mm resolution, the discernible upper limit is 0.1 mm and the discernible lower limit is 0.0 mm, then the best estimate is 0.05 mm with an uncertainty of 0.0289 mm.

If, from the measurement of a tipping bucket gauge with 1 mm resolution, the discernible upper limit is 1.0 mm and the discernible lower limit is 0.0 mm, then the best estimate is 0.5 mm with an uncertainty of 0.2887 mm.

### A.2.3 Float gauge

### A.2.3.1 Explanation

When rain enters the receiver to fill the cylinder, an observer records the precipitation on the recording paper of the rotational drum where the pen connected to the float interconnects with the magnetic clock. The gauge must have a large storage capacity to ensure 24 hours of continuous recording. When a certain amount of rain fills the storage, the storage should be emptied automatically in a short time frame to prevent the disconnection of recording for the precipitation measurement even when continuous heavy precipitation occurs.

NOTE 1 With advances in technology, some float gauges are fully digitalized and automated. This standard will not focus on non-recording float gauges since they are not commonly used.

Key

1 Funnel

2 Siphon

3 Float

4 Recording paper

5 Pen

Figure A.5 — Float gauge

### A.2.3.2 Material

The gauge should be manufactured with a durable and corrosion-free material like stainless steel.

### A.2.3.3 Advantages

It can measure in real-time, meaning that data are available for recording and/or transmittal through telemetry as soon as the measurement is obtained.

### A.2.3.4 Disadvantages

It is impossible to measure the precipitation during the siphon motion that drains the water. In cold temperatures, the possibility of frozen floats exists. It is necessary to have a steady power supply for recording and time meter motions.

### A.2.3.5 Uncertainties

Float gauges have a rectangular uncertainty distribution so that Equation (A.2) applies.

EXAMPLE	If, from the measurement, the discernible upper limit is 10 mm and the discernible lower limit is 9.9 mm, then the best estimate is 9.95 mm with an uncertainty of 0.0289 mm.

![image](image_036.png)

### File: ISO_FDIS 23350

ISO/FDIS 23350

ISO TC 113/SC 05/WG 7

Secretariat: ANSI

## Hydrometry—Catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauges

# FDIS stage

## Warning for WDs and CDs

This document is not an ISO International Standard. It is distributed for review and comment. It is

subject to change without notice and may not be referred to as an International Standard.

Recipients of this draft are invited to submit, with their comments, notification of any relevant patent rights of which they are aware and to provide supporting documentation.

### Contents

Introduction 	 3

1 Scope 	 4

2 Normative references 	 4

3 Terms and definitions 	 4

4 Instrument specifications 	 5

5 Recording 	 7

6 Environmental protection and housing 	 7

7 Installation 	 8

8 Estimation of measurement uncertainties 	 8

Annex A (Types of catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauges) 	 13

Annex B (Uncertainty of catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauges) 	 22

Bibliography 	 27

### Introduction

Rainfall totals and intensity rates are measured at frequencies ranging from seconds to months, and the data are required by the user on timescales ranging from the immediated to several weeks in arrears.

This document covers instrumentation that measures rainfall accumulation and rainfall intensity (RI) by collecting the volume of water that falls on a fixed area. The measurement unit and period of rainfall should be decided on gauge design and network purpose by the gauge user. Unlike many other meteorological instruments, there is no absolute physical standard against which a raingauge (hereafter called catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauges) can be compared. Many different types, shapes, and sizes of catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauges are acceptable for the measurement of rainfall and rainfall depth, each reflecting a specific requirement. Most consist of a circular collecting device, delineating the fixed area of the sample, and a funnel leading into a storage reservoir and/or measuring system. Some types of automatic gauges do not require a funnel. Since various sizes and shapes of orifice and gauge heights are used in different countries, the measurements are not strictly comparable. The purpose of this document is to provide general information on the functions of catching-type liquid precipitation gauges. The notes below provide references covering abroad range of specifications that are not could be included in future versions of this document.

NOTE 1 This document specifies only the general functions of catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauges, and does not include specification, design and installation conditions. For indepth and extensive information on precipitation measuring gauages, see WMO 8:12 and WMO 168.

Note 2 Detailed information on measurement unit and period of rainfall mentioned in the introduction are described in WMO 8:2018

Note 3 For detailed information on the design of a reference raingauge pit as well as

installation, see EN 13798:2010

(REFERENCE: BS 7843-3:2012)

(REFERENCE: WMO-No.8:2018)

## Hydrometry—Catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauges

### 1 Scope

This document specifies the typical requirements of instrumentation for measuring liquid precipitation, primarily for the purpose of hydrological and meteorological observation. Its scope is limited to both non-recording and recording catching-type precipitation gauges for the measurement of liquid precipitation. The standard covers design criteria for the gauges and elements of uncertainty that need to be considered in their construction.

This document is supplemented by an annex providing guidance on the types of catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauges currently available (see Annex A) and the measurement uncertainty associated with them (see Annex B). Although advances in measurement electronic technologies and smart instruments have led to the development of optical and hybrid type liquid precipitation measuring gauges, they are not included in this document but could be added in the future.

Note 1 Since the measurement of solid precipitation is outside the scope, it should be noted that the disadvantages of solid precipitation apply to all gauges listed in Annex A. The

measurement of solid precipitation and associated disadvantages are well documented in WMO 8:2018.

### 2 Normative references

The following referenced documents are indispensable for the application of this document. For dated references, only the edition cited applies. For undated references, the latest edition of the referenced document (including any amendments) applies.

ISO 772:2011, Hydrometry — Vocabulary and symbols

ISO/IEC Guide 98-3:2008, Guide to the expression of uncertainty in measurement (GUM:1995)

### 3 Terms and definitions

For the purpose of this document, the terms and definitions given in ISO 772 and BS 7843-3 apply.

### 4 Instrument specifications

### 4.1 General

Catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauges are classified as non-recording and recording types. The non-recording type has ordinary and storage gauges. The recording type has weighing, tipping-bucket, and floating types. Catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauges have a resolution of 0,1 mm to 1 mm, and can measure rainfall intensities of 0,1 to 1000 mm×h-1 or higher. Measurement errors may occur according to installation conditions, measurement environment, solid precipitation, and rainfall intensity.

The catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauge shall be made of circular orifice and shall be formed perpendicular to the outer slope and the inner surface with sharp edges. The diameter of the edge of the collecting device should not differ by more than 0.2 percent of the stated diameter in any direction. Furthermore, the collector should be designed to prevent rain from splashing in and out. This can be achieved if the vertical wall is sufficiently deep and the slope of the funnel is sufficiently steep (at least 45%). The container should have a narrow entrance and be sufficiently protected from radiation in minimize the loss of water by evaporation. (WMO 8:2018).The surface of the funnel shall consist of a stable, durable material such that the water drop is not retained by surface tension, is freely moved towards the orifice and is passed to the measuring mechanism.

NOTE 1 Although this document specifies the functional requirements of catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauges, consideration of the siting and exposure of gauges is important. More specifically, the installation conditions, measurement environment, and solid precipitation are well documented in the references below.

(REFERENCE: BS 7843-3:2012

(REFERENCE : WMO 8:2018)

### 4.2 General gauge specifications

The gauge manufacturer should provide general specifications that may impact the measurement of precipitation, these include but are not limited to:

a) Range

b) Resolution

c) Accuracy

d) Size of orifice area

e) Standardized communication interfaces and protocols for data transmission

f) Power supply and consumption

g) Height of gauge (including rim height placement to avoid interference from deposited snow or other materials near gauge)

i) Gauge materials

A broad statement of the requirements for uncertainty, range and resolution for precipitation measurements are well documented in WMO 08 (Annex 1.A.6). It should be be noted that in some countries, trace observations are officially given a value of zero, thus resulting in a biased underestimate of the seasonal precipititation. This problem is minimized with weighing-type gauges, since even very small amounts of precipitation will accumulate over time (WMO 82018).

### 4.3 Environment

### 4.3.1 General

Liquid precipitation measuring gauges shall operate within the ranges of temperature in 4.3.2 and the ranges of relative humidity in 4.3.3. Wind should be taken into consideration in areas that are prone to inclement weather conditions

(REFERENCE : WMO8:2018)

### 4.3.2 Temperature

Catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauges should function in their intended manner over the following environmental conditions:

a) Operating temperature for non-recording type and recording type gauges

- Maximum temperature : +60 °C

- Minimum temperature (catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauges, excluding heating or weighing gauge without antifreeze) : 0 °C

- Minimum temperature (catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauge, with heating or weighing gauge with antifreeze) : –40 °C

### 4.3.3 Relative humidity

Catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauges shall operate within the following relative humidity range:

Operating relative humidity for non-recording type and recording type gauges

- Maximum relative humidity : 100 % RH

- Minimum relative humidity : 0 % RH

(REFERENCE :BS 7843-3:2012)

### 5 Recording

### 5.1 Recording format

For recording and non-recording type liquid precipitation gauges, the time stamp of each record to be stored in a data storage gauge or manual recording shall be at least eight digits for the year, month and day, eight digits for the hour, minute, second and millisecond (e.g. 2019/12/31 09:00:00:00). For rainfall accumulation, there shall be at least five digits (e.g. 000,000 mm×h-1), not including the decimal place, for the liquid precipitation amount.

### 5.2 Recording interval

The recording interval for catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauges shall be specified by the network operator to ensure regional and climatological constraints are taken into consideration that could affect the accuracy and precision of recorded measurements

For recording type gauges, the measurement record of liquid precipitation should be transmitted to the recording device instantaneously or at a specified time according to the gauge user, the recording intervals are commonly hourly or every minute depending on gauge design and network purpose.

Non-recording type liquid precipitation gauges should be read manually at a specified interval to acquire the data.

Ordinary and Storage gauges should be emptied in line with network operating principles, commonly daily or monthly depending on gauge design and network purpose. Liquid precipitation is reported per unit of time, the manufacturer or network operator should specify the volume of liquid precipitation and time interval recorded in its most granular form.

### 6 Environmental protection and housing

The catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauge should be constructed in a material that is durable and can endure natural weathering without changes to its surface characteristics. Sound watertight seams should be used throughout. The collecting funnel should fit firmly over the top of the measurement mechanism but should be removable without undue force. The gauge should be designed to minimize ingress by small animals and insects. The outer housing of the gauge should also prevent water from entering the measuring component of the gauge, apart from through the inlet funnel. Electrical connections for power supplies and outgoing signals should be provided using water-resistant plugs or terminals, suitable for the environmental conditions in which the gauge is deployed. Ingress Protection Code 67 (IP67) is suitable for this. Any marking identifying connectors should be weather-resistant and remain legible for the expected lifetime of the catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauge.

(REFERENCE : IEC 60529:Edition 2.2 2013-08))

### 7 Installation

The manufacturer shall provide clear instructions for the installation of catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauges. As mentioned in Clause 4.1 of this document, the sitting and exposure of raingauges should be taken into consideration.

NOTE 1 Although outside the scope of this standard, it should be noted that measurements will be influenced by rim height either through individual gauge design or local deployment to allow for factors such as snow depth where close to ground level deployment is not practical. All gauge types should be sited to ensure operation within the manufacturer’s design.

The rim of the gauge should be installed parallel to the ground surface. The design should maintain plane of rim level to within ±2° of the base plane. Failure to ensure that the rim is level will introduce a systematic error into measurement. The measurement mechanism of the gauge, e.g. a tipping bucket mechanism, also needs to be level to ensure there are no systematic errors.

The gauge should be installed securely to ensure that it remains stable. This must take into account impacts such as frost heave and changes in ground conditions.

The gauge should be sited to ensure that the surrounding surface minimises splashback that can result in an over-catch. A surface of gravel or short grass is typical and surfaces such as concrete should be avoided.

(REFERENCE : BS 7843-3:2012)

### 8 Estimation of measurement uncertainty

### 8.1 General

The uncertainty of a value derived from primary measurements may be due to

a) Signal Noise which affects the value being measured (variation by the precipitation intensity), or

b) Resolution of the measurement process.

c) Other factors (wetting, splashing, evaporation, wind induced under-catch, volume-mass conversions, time interval, etc.)

(REFERENCE : WMO 168:2008)Two methods of estimation, Type A and Type B, are described in the Guide to the Expression of Uncertainty in Measurement (GUM 1995) for relating the dispersion of values to the probability of “closeness” to the mean value.

NOTE 1 Although installation and environmental factors are major sources of uncertainties, they are outside the scope

### 8.2 Factors of measurement uncertainty

In practice, there are many possible sources of uncertainty in a measurement, including

a) incomplete definition of the measurand;

b) imperfect realization of the definition of the measurand;

c) nonrepresentative sampling — the sample measured may not represent the defined measurand;

d) inadequate knowledge of the effects of environmental conditions on the measurement or imperfect measurement of environmental conditions;

e) personal bias in reading analogue instruments;

f) finite instrument resolution or discrimination threshold;

g) inexact values of measurement standards and reference materials;

h) inexact values of constants and other parameters obtained from external sources and used in the data-reduction algorithm;

i) approximations and assumptions incorporated in the measurement method and procedure;

j) variations in repeated observations of the measurand under apparently identical conditions.

(REFERENCE : GUM ISO/IEC Guide 98-3:2008)

(REFERENCE : WMO 8:2018)

### 8.3 Type-A estimation

It is the method of evaluation of uncertainty by the statistical analysis of series of observations. It stems from manual reading and evaporation and wetting of the instrument.

(REFERENCE : GUM ISO/IEC Guide 98-3:2008)

### 8.4 Type-B estimation

It is the method of evaluation of uncertainty by means other than the statistical analysis of series of observations. It stems from resolution, aging, and random error of the instrument.

(REFERENCE : GUM ISO/IEC Guide 98-3:2008)

### 8.5 Combined uncertainty

To determine the uncertainty of the derived value, U, it is necessary to combine the uncertainties of all primary measurements, u, thus,

$U_{c}=[u^{2}_{A}+u^{2}_{B}]^{\frac{1}{2}}$	(1)

Where

$U_{c}$ is combined uncertainty;

$u^{2}_{A}$ is the uncertainty of type-A;

$u^{2}_{B}$ is the uncertainty of type-B.

This illustrates the method when combining the uncertainty of a reference precipitation datum value. Other components of measurement uncertainty are added by inclusion of their squared value within the brackets.

(REFERENCE: GUM ISO/IEC Guide 98-3:2008)

### Annex A

(Types of catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauges)

### A.1 Non-recording gauges

### A.1.1 Ordinary gauge

### A.1.1.1 Explanation

The ordinary gauge is composed of the collector orifice, a storage with a funnel shape, and a bucket. The size of the opening of the collector important. A receiving area of 1000cm2 is used in some countries, but an area of 200 cm2 to 500 cm2 will probably be found most convenient. The area of the receiver may be made to equal .01 of the area of the collector. (WMO 168) Storing rain or solid precipitation during the observation hours, the graduated cylinder shall be used to measure the accumulated precipitation.

Key

1 Funnel

2 Measuring gauge

Figure A.1 — Ordinary gauge

(REFERENCE: https://geologyscience.com/applied-geology/hydrogeology/elements-of-the-hydrologic-cycle-1/)

### A.1.1.2 Materials

An ordinary gauge is made of various types of metal or plastic materials with proper thermal expansion coefficient. They must be manufactured to minimize errors due to wetting loss and evaporation.

(REFERENCE : WMO CIMO_Guide-7th_Edition-2008-WMO No 8, CH 6.3.1.1)

### A.1.1.3 Advantages

An ordinary gauge is easy to install and measure and is available to measure precipitation accurately at a low cost.

### A.1.1.4 Disadvantages

Ordinary gauges must be read manually and cannot determine the exact start and end of the precipitation occurrence. The timing cannot be determined between individual events within the recording period. Uncertainty factors exist due to measurement time and scale indication. Evaporation may result in under reading, particularly if measurements are made less frequently than daily.

### A.1.1.5 Uncertainties

The uncertainties of ordinary gauges are detailed in Annex B of this document.

### A.1.2 Storage gauge

### A.1.2.1 Explanation

An observer of a storage gauge can either measures the weight or read the graduated cylinder of a bucket after collecting rainwater in the bucket for a certain period such as measuring the total precipitation in a remote area.

The gauge is composed of a collecting part with a funnel and a storage part that can hold enough precipitation between scheduled readings. Proper oil of around 5 mm shall be typically put in a measuring bottle and a removable inner can for over spill to prevent the evaporation of water. This oil shall have a property to pass through collected water freely to prevent evaporation.

When the location of a storage gauge is located far from the observer, the gauge requires special care in terms of operation and management. For example, the installation of a video camera can provide valuable information about the conditions at a gauging site. The extent of ice cover, periods of backwater due to ice, etc., can be documented by a camera. (WMO 168 -2.5.8.5)

Key

1 Funnel

2 Bucket

Figure A.2 — Storage gauge

(REFERENCE: https://cliffmass.blogspot.com/2016/03/new-technology-rain-gauge.html)

### A.1.2.2 Materials

Storage gauges are made of various types of metal or plastic materials with proper thermal expansion coefficient. They must be manufactured to minimize the loss of water by the wet error and evaporation.

### A.1.2.3 Advantages

This type of gauge is easy to install and measure, and is available to accurately measure precipitation at a low cost.

### A.1.2.4 Disadvantages

As it is not possible to measure in real-time and there exist many cases of poor accessibility, the gauge requires monitoring to check if the the funnel is plugged In addition, this type of gauge requires oil or antifreeze to prevent evaporation, but such substances may cause environmental pollution.

Storage gauges must be read manually and cannot determine the exact start and end of the precipitation occurrence. The timing cannot be determined between individual events within the recording period. Uncertainty factors exist due to measurement time and scale indication. Evaporation may result in under reading, particularly if measurements are made less frequently than daily.

### A.1.2.5 Uncertainties

The uncertainties of storage gauges are detailed in Annex B of this document.

### A.2 Recording gauges

As the catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauge with automatic recording has a higher time resolution than manual measurements, it is possible to reduce evaporation and wetting loses.

Recording gauges generally used are the weighing type, tipping-bucket type, and floating type. Some weighing gauges can measure all types of precipitation without the need for additional heaters. Other recording-type liquid precipitation measuring gauges will require heaters fitted to the rim and/or funnel if they are required to measure frozen precipitation.

Alternative technologies for measuring precipitation without moving parts are emerging. These

include condenser gauges, atmospheric pressure converter, and optic or small radar gauges.

They provide electronic signals in response to the precipitation amount and/or intensity. At the time of issuing the standard these technologies are not sufficiently developed to include them

within the appendix.

Another important component of recording rain gauges is accurate time recording of the precipitation amount over a set interval. This will be carried out by a data logging device either within the gauge or through an external data logger. This is essential for providing accurate information on the timeliness of rainfall accumulation and calculation of rainfall intensity.

### A.2.1 Weighing gauge

### A.2.1.1 Explanation

A weighing gauge measures the weight continuously by a weight sensor or a load cell when rain or solid precipitation enters through the upper part of the receiver and fills the collection bucket that is placed inside the casing. The gauge consists of a collection bucket to receive water and a gauge to measure weight. The weighing gauge can measure precipitation in the form of solid precipitation. Its operation has an advantage in that unnecessary special preparation processes besides emptying the water in the collection bucket are not required. A weighing gauge has to be designed to prevent excessive evaporation losses, which may be reduced further by the addition of sufficient oil or other evaporation suppressing materials to form a film over the water surface (WMO 168 3.4.1). Evaporation doesn’t necessarily need to be prevented if the gauge software takes into account the weight of the gauge when making calculations.

Key1 Receiving funnel

2 Collection bucket

3 Balance

4 Casing

Figure A.3 — Weighing gauge

(REFERENCE: https://geologyscience.com/applied-geology/hydrogeology/elements-of-the-hydrologic-cycle-1/)

### A.2.1.2 Materials

The exterior of the gauge should be manufactured with a durable and corrosion-free material like stainless steel which is commonly used. It is important to review the reliability of power supply since the gauge records measurements automatically.

### A.2.1.3 Advantages

It is useful to measure both liquid and solid type precipitation such as snow, sleet, and hail. This gauge provides precise measurements with fine resolution. In addition, the recording-type weighing gauge will require less maintenance since it is has no funnel or moving mechanical parts since mechanical degradation has been significantly reduced. (WMO 8:2012 (Ch 6.5.1.3))

### A.2.1.4 Disadvantages

If the precipitation accumulated in the bucket exceeds the capacity of bucket, it shall be discharged automatically or manually. The automatic discharge type does not use antifreeze for its operation, but the manual discharge type needs antifreeze to minimize water inside the bucket from freezing. In addition, in case of manual discharge type, the capacity of the bucket must be large and it needs an evaporation suppressor or the gauge software.

The weighing gauge is not suitable to discern the types of precipitation. Errors may occur as the load cell can be affected by pressure from high winds. Another common fault with weighing-gauges is wind pumping. This usually occurs during high winds when turbulent air currents passing over and around the catchment container cause oscillations in the weighing mechanism. Errors associated with such anomalous recordings can be minimized by averaging readings over short time intervals usually ranging from 1 to 5 min. (WMO 8:2018)A.2.1.5 Uncertainties.

### A.2.1.5 Uncertainties

The uncertainties of weighing gauges are detailed in Annex B of this document.A.2.2 Tipping bucket gauge

### A.2.2 Tipping bucket gauge

### A.2.2.1 Explanation

Tipping bucket gauges measure the rate and accumulated total of precipitation. Water collected in the receiver fills one of the two calibrated buckets with a predetermined amount of water. Once the predetermined amount of water is collected in the bucket, it tips over on one side due to the weight of the water and the rainwater is drained. Tipping bucket gauges employ a contact closure (reed switch or relay contact), such that each tip produces an electrical impulse as a signal output. This output must be recorded by a data logger or and ADC (data acquisition system equipped with reed switch reading ports). This mechanism provides a continuous measurement without manual interaction.

Note For futher details on tipping bucket gauges, including the measurement of rainfall intensity, calibration, etc. see WMO 8:2018 (6.5.2)

Key

1 Funnel

2 Tipping bucket

3 Drain cylinder

4 5 Reed-relay switch Calibration adjuster

Figure A.4 — Tipping bucket gauge

(REFERENCE: https://geologyscience.com/applied-geology/hydrogeology/elements-of-the-hydrologic-cycle-1/)

### A.2.2.2 Materials

The gauge should be manufactured with a durable and corrosion-free material like stainless steel.

### A.2.2.3 Advantages

The gauge is relatively simple. As it is available for remote measurements and easy to produce digitized observation signals, the gauge has been widely used for automatic weather stations. It is suitable to measure the accumulated sum and rate of precipitation.

### A.2.2.4 Disadvantages

The gauge has some limitations in catching high-intensity precipitation and if the precipitation intensity increases, the accuracy of observed values declines. Although negligible, there is a possibility of evaporation loss in hot regions. It is impossible for the gauge to measure if counting elements fail such as a reed switch, etc. If it is a heated type gauge errors are possible due to the evaporation of water collected.

Although the time is short when the water is poured out from the bucket and returns to its original position, a small amount of precipitation could be missed during that time. Since a nonlinear error can occur during high rainfall intensities, the precision of the gauge cannot be guaranteed.

(REFERENCE : WMO CIMO_Guide-7th_Edition-2008-WMO No 8, CH 6.5.2)

### A.2.2.5 Uncertainties

The uncertainties of tipping bucket gauges are detailed in Annex B of this document.

### A.2.3 Float gauge

### A.2.3.1 Explanation

Float-type gauges include a funnel for collecting precipitation, a cylinder for storing precipitation, a float in the cylinder, and a recording device that measures and records the water level in the cylinder. The recording mechanism can be a pen trace on a rotating drum or shaft encoder and data logger. When the cylinder nears capacity, it’s contents are emptied automatically by a syphon in a short time frame to prevent the disconnection of recording for the precipitation measurement even when continuous heavy precipitation occurs.

NOTE 1 With advances in technology, some float gauges are fully digitalized and automated. This standard will not focus on non-recording float gauges since they are not commonly used.

Key

1 Funnel

2 Siphon

3 Float

4 5 Recording paper Pen

Figure A.5 — Float gauge

(REFERENCE: https://geologyscience.com/applied-geology/hydrogeology/elements-of-the-hydrologic-cycle-1/)

### A.2.3.2 Material

The gauge should be manufactured with a durable and corrosion-free material like stainless steel.

### A.2.3.3 Advantages

A float gauge can measure in real-time, meaning that data are available for recording and/or transmittal through telemetry as soon as the measurement is obtained.

### A.2.3.4 Disadvantages

A float gauge impossible to measure the precipitation during the siphon motion that drains the water. In cold temperatures, the possibility of frozen floats exists. It is necessary to have a steady power supply for recording and time meter motions.

### A.2.3.5 Uncertainties

The uncertainties of floating gauges are detailed in Annex B of this document.

### Annex B

(Uncertainty of catching-type liquid precipitation measuring gauges)

### B.1 Uncertainties of non-recording gauges

### B.1.1 Uncertainties of type-A

Type-A uncertainty is derived from the experimental standard deviation and arithmetic mean (or average) measured n times.

$x=\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}++x_{n}}{n}$ㆍㆍㆍ

$_{s=}^{}_{\frac{_{i}\sum _{-}^{n}_{1}(xi^{-}x)^{2}}{n-1}}^{}_{}^{\frac{1}{2}}$

$u_{A}=\frac{s}{\sqrt{n}}$

Where

$x$ is the arithmetic mean(or average);

$x_{n}$ is the measurement values;

$s$ is the experimental standard deviation;

$u_{A}$ is the uncertainty of type-A.

(REFERENCE: GUM ISO/IEC Guide 98-3:2008, CH 4.2)

(B.1)

### B.1.2 Uncertainties of type-B

The type-B uncertainty of the non-recording guage, possible to occur during the reading, can be represented by the equation (B.2), consdiering the uncertainty of the cross-sectional area of the rim, the uncertainty of the cross-sectional area of the collection chamber, and the uncertainty of the length of the measuring stick or graduated cylinder.

$u_{B}=[u^{2}_{a1}+u^{2}_{a2}+u^{2}_{l}]^{\frac{1}{2}}$	(B.2)

Where

$u_{B}$ is the type-B uncertainty of non-recording gauges;

$u_{a1}$ is the uncertainty of the cross-sectional area of the rim;

$u_{a2}$ is the uncertainty of the cross-sectional area of the collection chamber;

$u_{l}$ is the uncertainty of the length of the measuring stick or graduated cylinder.

The equation (B.2) represents triangular uncertainty distribution and the equation (B.3) can be derived therefrom as follows:

$u_{B}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{6}}\times \frac{(x+-x-)}{2}$

(B.3)

Where

$u_{B}$ is the type-B uncertainty of non-recording gauges;

$x_{+}$ is the discernible upper limit;

$x_{-}$ is the discernible lower limit.

(REFERENCE: GUM ISO/IEC Guide 98-3:2008, CH 4.3)

### B.1.3 Combined uncertainty

Combined uncertainty of the non-recording gauge is derived from relationships represented in the equation (B.1) for type-A uncertainty and the equation (B.3) for type-B uncertainty.

$U_{c}=[u^{2}_{A}+u^{2}_{B}]^{\frac{1}{2}}$

$=^{}_{}(\frac{s}{\sqrt{n}})^{2}+(\frac{1}{\sqrt{6}}\times \frac{(x+-x-)}{2})^{2}_{}^{\frac{1}{2}}$

Where

$U_{c}$ is combined uncertainty of non-recording gauge;

(B.4)

EXAMPLE

| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Measurement Value(mm) | 99 | 100 | 99 | 100 | 101 | 100 | 101 | 100 | 99 | 101 |

- $x$ : arithmetic mean (or average)$x=\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}++x_{n}}{n}$ㆍㆍㆍ

$\frac{99+100+99+100+101+100+101+100+99+101}{10}$ $=$

= 100

- $s$ : experimental standard deviation

$_{s=}^{}_{\frac{\sum _{i-}^{n}_{1}(xi^{-}x)^{2}}{n-1}}^{}_{}^{\frac{1}{2}}$

= 0,816 5

- $u_{A}$ : uncertainty of type-A

$u_{A}=$

$\frac{s}{\sqrt{n}}$

$=$

$\frac{0,8165}{\sqrt{10}}$

= 0,258 2

- $u_{B}$ : uncertainty of type-B$_{uB}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{6}}_{\times}\frac{(x+-x-)}{2}$

$=$

$\frac{1}{\sqrt{6}}\times \frac{(101-99)}{2}$

= 0,408 2

$=$ $[0,2582+0,4082]^{\frac{1}{2}}$= 0,483

### B.2 Uncertainties of recording gauges

### B.2.1 Uncertainties of type-A

Type-A uncertainty is derived from the experimental standard deviation and arithmetic mean (or average) measured n times.

$x=\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}++x_{n}}{n}$ㆍㆍㆍ

$_{s=}^{}_{\frac{_{i}\sum _{-}^{n}_{1}(xi-x)^{2}}{n-1}}^{}_{}^{\frac{1}{2}}$

$u_{A}=\frac{s}{\sqrt{n}}$

Where

$x$ is the arithmetic mean(or average);

$x_{n}$ is the measurement values;

$s$ is the experimental standard deviation;

$u_{A}$ is the uncertainty of type-A.

(REFERENCE: GUM ISO/IEC Guide 98-3:2008, CH 4.2)

(B.5)

### B.2.2 Uncertainties of type-B

The type-B uncertainty of the recording guage, possible to ocurr during the measurement can be represented by the equation (B.6), considering the the uncertainty of the recording or measuring device, the uncertainty of the timing device, and the uncertainty of the amount of evaporation and/or the amount of oil or antifreeze added to the gauge.

$uB=[u^{2}b1+u^{2}b2+u^{2}e]^{\frac{1}{2}}$	(B.6)

Where

$u_{B}$ is the type-B uncertainty of recording gauges;

$u_{b1}$ is the uncertainty of the recording or measuring device;

$u_{b2}$ is the uncertainty of the timing device;

$u_{e}$ is the uncertainty of the amount of evaporation and/or the amount of oil or antifreeze added to the gauge.

The equation (B.6) represents rectangular uncertainty distribution and the equation (B.7) can be derived therefrom as follows:

$u_{B}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\times \frac{(x+-x-)}{2}$

(B.7)

Where

$u_{B}$ is the type-B uncertainty of recording gauges;

$x_{+}$ is the discernible upper limit;

$x_{-}$ is the discernible lower limit.

(REFERENCE: GUM ISO/IEC Guide 98-3:2008, CH 4.3)

### B.2.3 Combined uncertainty

Combined uncertainty of the recording gauge is derived from relationships represented in the equation (B.5) for type-A uncertainty and the equation (B.7) for type-B uncertainty.

$Uc=[u^{2}A+u^{2}B]^{\frac{1}{2}}$

$=^{}_{}(\frac{s}{\sqrt{n}})^{2}+(\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\times ^{\frac{(x+-x-)}{2}})^{2}_{}^{\frac{1}{2}}$

Where

$U_{c}$ is combined uncertainty of recording gauge;

(B.8)

### 참고문헌

## 서 론

강우 총량과 강도 비율은 초에서 월에 이르는 빈도로 측정되며, 데이터는 사용자가 즉시에서 몇 주 지연된 시간 범위로 요구해야 한다. 이 문서에서는 고정된 영역에 떨어지는 물의 양을 수집하여 강우 누적 및 강우 강도(RI)를 측정하는 계측기를 다룬다. 수집된 강우량(총액체 강수량)은 지표면의 수평 투영을 덮을 수 있는 물의 수직 깊이(일반적으로 밀리미터, mm)로 표현되며, mm⋅m-2 또는 kg⋅m-2 로 측정된다. 다른 많은 기상학적 기기와 달리, 절대적인 물리적 표준은 다시 존재하지 않는다. 어떤 강수량계(이하 집수형 액체 강수량 측정장치라고 함)를 비교할 수 있는가. 집수형 액체강수 측정장치의 다양한 유형, 형태 및 크기는 강우량 및 강우 깊이를 측정하는데 허용되며, 각각은 특정 요건을 반영한다. 대부분은 샘플의 고정된 영역을 설명하는 원형 수집 장치와 저장 용기 및/또는 측정 시스템으로 이 어지는 깔때기 등으로 구성된다. 일부 자동 측정장치는 깔때기가 필요하지 않다. 다양한 크기와 모양의 오리피스 및 측정장치 높이가 국가마다 사용되기 때문에 측정치는 엄격하게 비교되지 않는다.

참고 1 본 문서는 장치형 액체 강수량 측정장치의 일반적인 기능만을 명시하며 규격, 설계 및 설치 조건은 포함하지 않는다. 강수량 측정장치에 대한 심층적이고 광범위한 정보는 WMO 8:12 및 WMO 168을 참조한다.

참고 2 고체 강수량의 측정치는 범위를 벗어나므로, 고체 강수량의 단점이 부속서 A에 열거된 모든 측정장치에 적용된다는 점에 유의해야 한다. 고체 강수량 및 관련 단점의 측정은 WMO 8:2018에 잘 설명되어 있다.

참고 3 기준 피트 강수량계 설계 및 설치에 대한 자세한 내용은 EN 13798:2010을 참조한다.

## (참조: BS 7678-3:2012, Introduction)

(참조: WMO-No.:2012, CH 6.1.2 & CH 6.1.4.1) (참조: WMO-No.:2012, CH 6.1.2 & CH 6.1.4.1)

### 유량 측정 – 집수형 액체 강수량 측정장치

1 범위

본 국제 표준은 주로 수리학적 또는 기상학적 관측의 목적으로 수행하는 강수량 측정에 대한 일반적인 요구사항을 명시하고 있다. 그 범위는 강수량의 측정을 위한 비-기록 방식과 기록 방식의 집수형 강수량계로 제한된다. 이 표준은 강수량계와 강수량계의 구조에서 고려 해야 할 요소들에 대한 설계 기준을 다루고 있다.

이 국제 표준은 현재 사용되는 집수형 강수량계의 종류 및 이러한 강수량계와 관련된 측정 불확도에 대한 지침을 제공하는 부속서가 첨부되어 있다 (부속서 A 참조).

2 인용표준

다음에 인용된 문서들은 본 문서를 활용할 때 반드시 필요한 문서이다. 인용 문서에 날짜가 명시된 경우, 언급된 판본의 내용만 적용된다. 날짜가 명시되지 않은 문서의 경우에는, 인용 문서의 최신 판본(모든 개정사항 포함)만 적용된다.

ISO 772:2011, Hydrometry — Vocabulary and symbols

ISO/IEC Guide 98-3:2008, Guide to the expression of uncertainty in measurement (GUM:1995)

JCGM 200:2012 (E/F) International guide of metrology – Basic and general concepts and associated terms (VIM) 3rd Edition: 2008

3 용어 및 정의

본 문서에는 ISO 772, BS 7843-3, ISO/IEC Guide 98-3에 명시된 용어와 정의가 적용된다.

3.1 보정

특정한 조건에서 관련된 측정 불확도에 따라 집수형 강수량계가 표시하는 값과 측정 기준에 명시되어 있는 상응하는 기준값 사이의 관계를 정의하는 과정

(참고문헌: BS 7843-3:2012, CH 3.1)

3.2 강수 (액체 및 고체)

강수는 구름으로부터 떨어지는 수증기가 응축되거나 공기 중에서 지면을 향해 아래로 가라 앉으면서 만들어지는 액체 또는 고체 생성물이다. 강수에는 비, 우박, 눈, 이슬, 서리, 동상 수우(樹雨: 강수성 안개) 등이 있다.

(참고문헌: WMO No.8 :2012, CH 6.1.1)

3.3 강우량

대기 중의 응축을 포함한 강수량을 집수형 강수량계를 사용하여 측정한 전체 액체 성분의 양

## 3.4 강우 강도

단위 시간당 누적 강수

## (참고문헌: BS 7843-3:2012, CH 3.9)

## 3.5 우량계

강우량을 측정하기 위한 장치

## 3.5.1 자동 우량계

자동적인 수단을 동원하여 빗물을 측정하는 집수형 우량계 (참고문헌: BS 7843-3:2012, CH 3.11.1)

## 4 계측기기 규격

## 4.1 일반사항

집수형 강수량계는 비-기록 방식(보통우량계)과 기록 방식(자기우량계)으로 분류된다. 비-기록 방식은 보통우량계와 저수우량계다. 기록 방식은 질량 우량계, 전도식 우량계, 부자식 우량계가 있다. 이러한 우량계는 대개 0.1 mm \~ 1 mm의 측정 분해능을 가지고 있으며, 0.1 \~ 1.000 mm/h 또는 그 이상의 강수 강도를 측정한다. 설치 조건, 측정 환경, 고체 강수 여 부에 따라 측정 오차가 발생할 수도 있다.

집수형 강수량계는 원형 오리피스를 가지고 있으며, 외측 경사면과 날카로운 가장자리를 가진 내부 표면에 대하여 수직인 형태여야 한다. 가장자리 부분이 차지하는 직경은 모든 방향에서 전체 직경의 0.2% 미만이어야 한다. 깔때기의 표면은 매끄럽고 안정적이며 내구성이 뛰어난 물질로 제작되어, 표면 장력에 의해 표면에 물방울이 남아 있지 않고 오리피스를 향해 자유롭게 흘러내려 강수량 측정 메커니즘을 통과할 수 있어야 한다.

비고 1. 이 표준은 집수형 강수량계의 기능적인 요구사항을 명시하고 있지만, 장치의 설치 위치와 노출을 고려하는 것도 중요하다. 좀 더 구체적으로, 아래의 참고문헌에서 설치 조건, 측정 환경, 고체 강수에 대한 사항을 잘 설명하고 있다.

(참고문헌: BS 7843-3:2012, CH 7.2.3)

(참고문헌: BS 7843-3:2012, CH 6.1.5)

## (참고문헌: WMO CIMO_Guide-7th_Edition-2008-WMO No 8, CH 6)

## 4.2 일반적인 우량계 규격

우량계 제조업체는 강수 측정에 영향을 미칠 수도 있는 부분에서 일반적인 규격을 준수해야 한다. 이러한 규격은 아래와 같으나, 여기에 포함되지 않은 내용도 있을 수 있다:

- 강수 강도 또는 충 강우량의 측정 범위

## - 우랑계의 분해능(resolution)

- 정확도

- 오리피스 부분의 크기

- 데이터 전송을 위한 표준화된 통신 인터페이스와 프로토콜

- 전원과 전력 소비량

- 우량계의 높이 (지면에 쌓인 눈이나 계측기기 인근의 다른 물질의 간섭을 피하기 위해 필요한 림(테두리)의 높이 포함)

## 4.3 환경

## 4.3.1 일반

강수량계는 4.3.2에 명시된 온도 범위 이내에서 작동해야 하며 4.3.3의 상대습도 범위에서 작동해야 한다.

비고 1. 기상 조건이 악화되기 쉬운 장소에서는 풍향을 고려해야 한다.

(참고문헌: WMO Guide to Meteorological Instruments and Methods of Observation, PART 1)

## 4.3.2 온도

집수형 강수량계는 다음과 같은 온도 범위에서 올바르게 작동해야 한다:

a) 작동 온도

## - 최대 온도: + 60 °C

- 최소 온도 (히터가 없는 집수형 강수량계 또는 부동액을 사용하지 않는 질량 우량계): 0 °

- 최소 온도 (히터가 있는 집수형 강수랑계 또는 부동액을 사용하는 질량 우량계): - 40 °C

## 4.3.3 상대습도

집수형 강수량계는 다음과 같은 상대습도 범위 이내에서 작동해야 한다:

a) 작동 상대습도

- 최대 상대습도: 100 % RH

- 최소 상대습도: 0 % RH

## (참고문헌: BS 7843-3:2012, CH 11.1\~ 11.2)

## 5 기록

## 5.1 기록 형식

기록 방식과 비-기록 방식 강수량계에서, 데이터 저장소에 저장되거나 수동으로 기록되는 각 기록의 길이는 연, 월과 일을 나타내는 8자리 숫자와 시, 분, 초, 밀리초를 나타내는 8자리 숫자로 구성되어야 한다 (예: 2019/12/31 09:00:00:00). 강우강도(RI)의 경우, 최소한 5 자리 이상으로 작성해야 하며(예: 00.000 mm/h), mm/h 또는 kg/m2 단위로 측정되는 강수량의 경우에는 소수점 이하는 포함하지 않는다.

## 5.2 기록 간격

네트워크 운영자는 기록 간격을 명시하여 규제를 준수하고 모든 지역적 제약 또는 기후학적 제한에 맞추어야 한다.

기록 방식의 강수량계의 경우, 강수량 측정 기록을 기록 장치로 즉시 전송하거나 우량계 사용자가 지정한 시간에 전송해야 한다.

비-기록 방식의 강수량계의 경우 지정된 간격마다 수동으로 계측하여 자료를 수집해야 한다.

보통 우량계나 저장식 우량계는 네트워크 작동 원리에 따라 우량계를 비워야 하며, 일반적으로 게이지 설계나 네트워크의 목적에 따라 하루에 한 번 또는 한 달에 한 번씩 비워야 한다. 강수량은 단위 시간당 강수량으로 기록되며, 제조업체 또는 네트워크 운영자는 가장 상세한 형식으로 강수량과 기록 시간 간격을 지정해야 한다.

## 6 환경 보호와 장비의 케이스

집수형 강수량계는 내구성이 뛰어난 재질로 만들어야 하며, 표면 특성의 변화 없이 자연적인 풍화 작용을 견딜 수 있어야 한다. 신뢰할 수 있는 장치 전체에는 방수 처리가 적용되어야 한다. 집수 깔때기는 측정 메커니즘의 상단에 단단히 장착되어야 하지만 과도한 힘을 들이지 않고도 제거할 수 있어야 한다. 강수량계는 작은 동물이나 곤충의 침투를 최소화하도록 설계되어야 한다. 또한, 장치의 외부 케이스는 주입구 깔때기를 통해 들어오는 물을 제 외한 다른 모든 물이 장치 부품 안으로 들어오는 것을 방지해야 한다. 장치가 설치되는 장소의 환경 조건에 적합한 방수 플러그 또는 단자를 사용하여 전원 공급과 외부 신호 전송을 위한 전기 연결을 제공해야 한다. 이러한 목적을 위해서는 IP67방진/방수 보호 규격이 적합하다. 커넥터의 식별을 위한 모든 표시는 날씨에 마멸되지 않도록 저항성이 있어야 하며, 집수형 강수량계의 예상 사용 수명 동안 읽을 수 있어야 한다.

## (참고문헌: IEC 60529: Edition 2.2 2013-08, CH 13\~14)

## 7 설치

제조업체는 집수형 강수량계의 설치에 대하여 명확하고 이해하기 쉽게 설명해야 한다.

비고 1. 비록 이 표준의 범위에 포함되지는 않지만, 장치의 설계나 장치를 설치하는 지역에 따라 림 높이가 측정값에 영향을 미칠 수도 있다는 점을 언급할 필요가 있다 (예를 들면, 지면에 가까이 설치하기에 적합하지 않은 높은 적설량과 같은 요소들을 고려해야 함). 모든 종류의 강수량계는 제조업체가 지정한 설계 수치 이내로 작동할 수 있는 장소에 배치해야 한다.

강수량계의 림 부분은 지면과 평행하게 설치해야 한다. 장치 설계는 림 높이의 평면이 바닥 면과 ±2° 미만의 각도를 유지하도록 해야 한다. 림의 수평을 맞추는데 실패하는 경우, 측정값에서 계통적 오차가 발생할 수 있다. 강수량계의 측정 메커니즘(예: 전도식(tipping bucket) 메커니즘) 또한 수평을 맞추어서 계통적 오차가 발생하지 않도록 해야 한다.

강수량계는 안정적인 상태를 유지하도록 단단하게 고정하여 설치해야 한다. 이를 위해서는 동상 현상(frost heave)이나 지면 상태의 변화가 측정에 미치는 영향을 고려해야 한다.

강수량계는 실제보다 더 많은 양의 물이 모이게 되는 원인인 주위 지표면의 물 튐 현상을 최소화할 수 있는 장소에 설치해야 한다. 자갈이나 짧은 풀이 자란 지표면에 설치하는 것이 일반적이며 콘크리트와 같은 표면은 피해야 한다. (참고문헌: BS 7843-3:2012, CH 6.1.4)

8 측정 불확도의 추정

8.1 일반사항

1차 측정에서 얻은 값의 불확도는 다음과 같은 원인에 의해 발생할 수 있다

a) 측정되는 값에 영향을 미치는 신호 잡음 (강수강도에 의한 변화)

b) 측정 과정의 분해능(resolution)

평균값에 “가장 가까울” 확률에 대한 분산 값에 대해 다룬 Guide to the Expression of Uncertainty in Measurement (GUM 1995)에서는 A형과 B형이라는 두 가지 불확도 예측 방법을 설명하였다.

참고 1. 설치나 환경적 요소가 불확도의 주요 원인이기는 하지만, 이러한 요소는 본 문서의 범위에 포함되지 않는다.

8.2 측정 불확도의 요소

실제로 측정의 불확실성에는 다양한 원인이 있을 수 있다:

a) 계측값에 대한 관측자의 편향

b) 계측기기의 한정된 분해능(resolution) 또는 구분 한계

c) 측정량의 반복적 관찰에서 발생하는 변화

(참고문헌: GUM ISO/IEC Guide 98-3:2008, CH 3.3)

예 작동 측정 불확도 요구사항 및 계측기 성능 요구사항

| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
